Like, I’m in school, and I also have this little accessories shop I’m trying to run, and it’s like I can’t keep both of them straight at the same time.\n\nMy supplies end up everywhere, and then I have due dates for school, receipts for the shop, assignment notes, little lists of what I need to make or buy, and somehow they all turn into these piles. Not always in a dramatic way, but people have definitely noticed that I’m not reliable with certain things.\n\nLike with my school program, there were these required steps I was supposed to complete, and I just… I missed them. Not because I didn’t want to do it, but because the information was in different places and I kept thinking I’d come back to it, and then suddenly it was like, “Wait, that was due?” And now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary and embarrassing.\n\nAnd with the shop stuff, when I’ve worked on projects with other people, I can tell they don’t depend on me as much anymore. Like they’ll take over parts I was supposed to handle or double-check everything, because I’ve misplaced things or forgotten steps or not followed through the way I meant to. I don’t think they hate me or anything, but it feels like they trust me less, and that’s been really upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but if I’m sitting in a lecture or a study group meeting, my leg is usually bouncing like crazy under the table. Or I’ll be clicking a pen, and then I realize it’s probably annoying someone, so I stop… but then two minutes later I’m messing with something else.\n\nI’ll dig in my bag and start playing with beads or a key ring or whatever random thing is in there, and sometimes I’m doing it before I even realize my hands are moving. It’s like my body is trying to keep itself busy while I’m supposed to be paying attention. I’m not trying to be distracting, and I do feel embarrassed if someone notices, but sitting still and just focusing quietly feels really uncomfortable for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. The main place I notice it is in class discussions or critiques. Like, if someone is giving feedback or the professor is asking a question, I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished talking. In my head I’m already like, “Oh, I know where this is going,” and I answer based on what I think they’re about to say.\n\nAnd then sometimes they finish and I realize I guessed wrong, or I interrupted the point they were still trying to make. It’s really embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude or take over, it just comes out so fast. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Why didn’t I just wait two more seconds?” But in the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose the thought or miss the chance.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling extremely upset or angry for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of it coming out of nowhere, like suddenly being furious and not knowing why. I mean, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but usually I can point to something that set it off, even if it’s kind of a small thing or I’m embarrassed that it bothered me that much.\n\nAngry for no reason doesn’t really sound like me. It’s more like if I’m upset, I usually know what it’s connected to after I think about it for a second. So… no, I wouldn’t say that happens often.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a hard time waiting for your turn in line or when participating in group activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Waiting my turn is honestly one of the things that makes me feel kind of… physically impatient, if that makes sense. Like in critiques, when we’re going around and people are talking, I’ll have something I want to say and I can feel myself getting all wound up waiting for the right moment. I’ll shift in my chair, change positions, mess with my stuff, and I’m trying to look normal, but inside I’m like, “Okay, okay, when can I say it?”\n\nAnd sometimes I cut in before I mean to. Not always in a super obvious way, but I’ll start talking right as someone else is still finishing, or I’ll jump in because I think there’s a pause and then realize it wasn’t really my turn yet. It’s embarrassing because I don’t want to seem like I think what I’m saying is more important.\n\nLines are bad too, especially financial aid lines at school. Those feel like they take forever, and I’ll keep shifting from foot to foot, checking how many people are ahead of me, looking around, kind of getting irritated even if nothing is actually wrong. Same thing after class if I need to ask the professor a question and other people are waiting. I’ll feel this pressure to get it out, and sometimes I step in too soon or start talking before the other person is fully done. I really do try to wait, but it takes a lot of effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes when working on tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a big, obvious pattern, like constantly messing things up because I missed details. I mean, I’m sure I make mistakes sometimes, like anyone does, but I can’t immediately think of a specific kind of task where I’m always making careless mistakes or skipping over details.\n\nWhen you say details, do you mean like schoolwork and assignments, or more like everyday things? Because if it’s something specific, maybe I could think about it better, but just generally… I don’t know that I’d say yes strongly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused when you're reading or doing quiet activities by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen. Not every single time I read or do something quiet, but pretty often, especially if it’s for school or like my business assignments.\n\nLike if I’m in class, I can start out really trying to listen, and at first I’m following what the professor is saying. But then a few minutes later I’ll realize my brain kind of wandered off without me noticing. I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or what kind of orders I could make, or ideas for how I want something to look, and then suddenly I’m like, wait, what did they just say? And it’s not that I decided to stop listening, it just sort of happens.\n\nIt’s the same with videos I’m supposed to watch for assignments. I’ll sit down and press play and genuinely mean to pay attention, but then after a little bit I notice I missed a whole chunk of it. Sometimes I have to rewind because I realize I heard the sound but didn’t actually take in what they were explaining. It’s kind of frustrating because I’m physically there, and I’m not trying to ignore it, but my mind is already somewhere else making plans or coming up with ideas.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects that you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not always that I just abandon the whole thing, but I’ll start something and feel like I’m doing it, and then later realize I didn’t actually finish all the parts I was supposed to.\n\nLike if a teacher gives me a few steps for an assignment, I’ll do the first thing or the main part, and then I’ll find out I skipped something important, like attaching the form or turning in an extra piece that was part of the instructions. Or with vendor stuff, if I’m talking to someone about an order and they give me a couple of details to include, I’ll get the basic note down, but then later realize I didn’t finish the last part of the order note or left out something they specifically asked for.\n\nIt’s frustrating because in my head I feel like, “Okay, I started it, I’m handling it,” but then the follow-through part gets messy. I don’t always notice until someone points it out or I’m already past the point where it would’ve been easier to fix.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to stick with a task until it's fully completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I think that’s kind of one of the bigger problems for me, honestly. It’s like I can start something and I’ll feel like I’m doing it properly, but sticking with it all the way through every step is where it gets messy.\n\nLike if a teacher gives me a few things I’m supposed to do for an assignment, I’ll usually do the first part, or the part that feels most obvious, and then later I’ll find out I missed something important. Like I forgot to attach a form, or I didn’t do one of the extra pieces that was part of the instructions. And it’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, I don’t care about that part.” I just sort of move forward and then realize afterward that I didn’t actually finish the whole thing.\n\nIt happens with vendor stuff too. If I’m in a meeting or talking about an order and they give me several details, I’ll write down or remember the main thing, but then later I notice I didn’t finish the last part of the order note, or I left out something they specifically wanted included. So in my head it feels like I handled it, but then when I look back, it’s like… no, I only handled part of it. And that’s frustrating because it makes me feel careless even when I was trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like at home or in a library?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I notice it a lot when I’m supposed to be sitting still, especially if it’s quiet and there isn’t anything I’m actively doing with my hands or body.\n\nIt’s hard to explain, but even if I’m not actually going anywhere or needing to do anything, my body feels like it’s buzzing almost. Like there’s this feeling inside me that I should be moving somehow. If I’m sitting and listening, I’ll feel like I need to shift around, bounce my leg, move my hands, adjust how I’m sitting, anything. And if I try not to, then I’m just thinking about how badly I want to move, so it’s not really restful.\n\nIt happens at home too, not just in public. Like if I’m trying to sit quietly or someone is talking and I’m supposed to just listen, I can feel myself getting restless even if nothing is actually wrong. It’s not always that I get up and leave, but I feel like I have all this energy trapped in my body and I need to do something with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble controlling your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, I have emotions like anyone, and sometimes I get upset or frustrated, but I don’t know if I’d say I “often” have trouble controlling them.\n\nMaybe it depends what you mean by controlling them? Like, if you mean do I have huge outbursts all the time or I can’t calm down at all, I don’t think that’s really me. But if you mean do I sometimes feel something strongly and it shows on my face or in my tone before I’ve fully thought it through, then… maybe sometimes? I don’t know if that’s more than normal, though.\n\nI guess I haven’t really paid attention to it as a main problem. I’d probably need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting overly emotional or reactive to situations?\n\nPatient: I’m still not completely sure, honestly. “Overly emotional” is hard for me to judge because I don’t always know what the normal amount of reaction is supposed to be in a situation.\n\nI don’t think I’m constantly having big reactions or anything like that. Like, I wouldn’t say people are always telling me I’m too emotional or that I’m blowing things out of proportion. But sometimes if something catches me off guard or frustrates me, I might react kind of quickly in the moment before I’ve had time to think it through. Then afterward I might be like, okay, maybe I sounded more irritated than I meant to, or maybe I took that more personally than I needed to.\n\nBut I don’t know if that happens often enough that I’d call it a major problem. It’s not really something I’ve tracked. I guess I’d say… maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d describe myself as often overly emotional or reactive.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering away from the task at hand when you're supposed to be focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. I can start out really trying to pay attention, like in class or if I’m watching a video for one of my business assignments, and at first I’m listening and following along. But then after a few minutes, it’s like my brain just slips off somewhere else without me deciding to.\n\nI’ll suddenly be thinking about charm designs, or ideas for orders, or like what colors would look good together, or how I could organize something for that. And it’s not always totally random stuff, it’s just not what I’m supposed to be focusing on at that moment.\n\nThen I kind of snap back and realize the video has kept going or the teacher has been talking, and I missed a whole chunk of what was said. Sometimes it feels like I was technically sitting there the whole time, looking like I was paying attention, but I wasn’t really taking it in. So then I have to rewind the video or try to piece together what I missed, which is frustrating because I did sit down intending to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick with one activity for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can stick with something if it’s really grabbing me, but if it’s something like class or a video I’m supposed to watch for my business assignments, it gets hard pretty quickly.\n\nI’ll start off fine, like I’m listening and trying to follow what they’re saying, and then after a few minutes I realize my brain has gone somewhere else completely. Not even always somewhere random — it’ll be like charm designs, or ideas for orders, or colors I want to use, or something related to that. And then I’ll kind of come back and notice the video is still going or the teacher has moved on, and I missed like half of what was said.\n\nSo it’s not always that I get up and stop the activity physically, but mentally I’m not really staying with it for that long. I have to rewind things or try to catch back up, and it can make something take way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to maintain your focus on a single task for as long as it requires to be completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. That’s probably one of the more noticeable things for me, especially with school-type stuff or anything I have to sit through until it’s done.\n\nLike if I’m in class, or I’m watching a video for my business assignments, I can start out actually listening. I’m not trying to ignore it. I’ll be like, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I need this information. But then after a few minutes, my mind just kind of drifts off before I even realize it’s happening. I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or order ideas, or what colors I want to use for something, and it feels like I’ve gone down this whole separate train of thought.\n\nThen I’ll suddenly notice the teacher is still talking, or the video has kept playing, and I’ve missed half of what was said. Sometimes I’m still looking at the screen, so it feels weird because physically I’m there, but mentally I’m not really following it anymore. Then I have to rewind or try to figure out what I missed, which makes the whole task take longer than it should. So yeah, maintaining focus all the way until something is finished is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your attention on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I know I kind of said this already, but it’s really like… I can begin something with good intentions, like in class or watching one of the videos I have to watch for my business assignments, and for the first few minutes I’m actually there. I’m listening, I’m following what’s being said, I’m trying to take it in.\n\nBut then after a little bit, my attention just sort of slides away. I’ll suddenly be thinking about charm designs, or ideas for orders, or colors I want to use, and I don’t even always notice right when it happens. It’s like I’m still sitting there and maybe even looking at the screen, but my brain has gone somewhere completely different.\n\nThen I’ll snap back and realize I missed a big part of what the teacher said, or half the video went by and I don’t really know what happened. So keeping my attention on one thing for a long time is hard, especially if it’s something I have to just sit and listen to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with friendships, work, or other activities, because of your organizational issues or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has started causing real problems, not just like “oh, I’m a little scattered.” The biggest thing is with my program. There were required steps I was supposed to keep up with, and I missed some of them because I wasn’t organized about it and didn’t stay on top of what needed to be done. And now my financial aid is at risk, which is honestly really stressful because it feels like one of those things where I knew it mattered, but I still didn’t manage it the way I should have.\n\nAnd then with shop projects, it’s affected things too. Like people I work with have started depending on me less, I think because they’re not always sure I’m going to follow through smoothly or keep track of everything. That feels embarrassing, because I do care about the work and I don’t want people to feel like they can’t count on me. But I can tell it’s changed a little, like they’ll take over parts or not ask me for as much, and I can’t really blame them, but it still feels bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulties with organization, restlessness, or completing tasks affect your performance or relationships not just in school or your shop, but also in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not just me being messy or forgetting something small — it’s like, okay, this could actually affect whether I can continue the way I planned. And that’s been stressful because I knew those things mattered, but I still didn’t stay organized enough to handle them properly.\n\nAnd with shop projects, it’s affected relationships too, I guess. People I work with have started depending on me less, like they don’t fully trust that I’ll keep track of everything or finish my part smoothly. I can kind of feel them pulling back or taking over things, and that’s embarrassing because I really do care. I don’t want to seem unreliable. So yeah, even if I mostly notice it in those areas, it does affect how people see me and how much they count on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think the organization and finishing things causes more obvious problems, like consequences people can point to. But the restlessness is really bothersome too, just in a different way.

It’s like even when I’m not actually going anywhere or doing anything, my body feels kind of… buzzy? Like there’s this energy under my skin and I’m supposed to move somehow. Especially if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m expected to just be still and listen, it gets really uncomfortable. I’ll feel like I need to shift around or do something with my hands or change position, and if I try not to, then I’m focusing on trying to stay still instead of whatever I’m supposed to be listening to.

So I guess the organization stuff messes things up more on the outside, but the restlessness is more like this constant inside irritation that makes sitting through things feel harder than it probably should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I came in because I feel like I’m kind of drowning in trying to keep things organized. Not because I didn’t want to do it, but because the information was in different places and I kept thinking I’d come back to it, and then suddenly it was like, “Wait, that was due?” And now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary and embarrassing.\n\nAnd with the shop stuff, when I’ve worked on projects with other people, I can tell they don’t depend on me as much anymore. I don’t think they hate me or anything, but it feels like they trust me less, and that’s been really upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but if I’m sitting in a lecture or a study group meeting, my leg is usually bouncing like crazy under the table. Or I’ll be clicking a pen, and then I realize it’s probably annoying someone, so I stop… but then two minutes later I’m messing with something else.\n\nI’ll dig in my bag and start playing with beads or a key ring or whatever random thing is in there, and sometimes I’m doing it before I even realize my hands are moving. It’s like my body is trying to keep itself busy while I’m supposed to be paying attention. I’m not trying to be distracting, and I do feel embarrassed if someone notices, but sitting still and just focusing quietly feels really uncomfortable for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. The main place I notice it is in class discussions or critiques. Like, if someone is giving feedback or the professor is asking a question, I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished talking. In my head I’m already like, “Oh, I know where this is going,” and I answer based on what I think they’re about to say.\n\nAnd then sometimes they finish and I realize I guessed wrong, or I interrupted the point they were still trying to make. It’s really embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude or take over, it just comes out so fast. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Why didn’t I just wait two more seconds?” But in the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose the thought or miss the chance.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling extremely upset or angry for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of it coming out of nowhere, like suddenly being furious and not knowing why. I mean, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but usually I can point to something that set it off, even if it’s kind of a small thing or I’m embarrassed that it bothered me that much.\n\nAngry for no reason doesn’t really sound like me. It’s more like if I’m upset, I usually know what it’s connected to after I think about it for a second. So… no, I wouldn’t say that happens often.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a hard time waiting for your turn in line or when participating in group activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Waiting my turn is honestly one of the things that makes me feel kind of… physically impatient, if that makes sense. Like in critiques, when we’re going around and people are talking, I’ll have something I want to say and I can feel myself getting all wound up waiting for the right moment. I’ll shift in my chair, change positions, mess with my stuff, and I’m trying to look normal, but inside I’m like, “Okay, okay, when can I say it?”\n\nAnd sometimes I cut in before I mean to. Not always in a super obvious way, but I’ll start talking right as someone else is still finishing, or I’ll jump in because I think there’s a pause and then realize it wasn’t really my turn yet. It’s embarrassing because I don’t want to seem like I think what I’m saying is more important.\n\nLines are bad too, especially financial aid lines at school. Those feel like they take forever, and I’ll keep shifting from foot to foot, checking how many people are ahead of me, looking around, kind of getting irritated even if nothing is actually wrong. Same thing after class if I need to ask the professor a question and other people are waiting. I’ll feel this pressure to get it out, and sometimes I step in too soon or start talking before the other person is fully done. I really do try to wait, but it takes a lot of effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes when working on tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a big, obvious pattern, like constantly messing things up because I missed details. I mean, I’m sure I make mistakes sometimes, like anyone does, but I can’t immediately think of a specific kind of task where I’m always making careless mistakes or skipping over details.\n\nWhen you say details, do you mean like schoolwork and assignments, or more like everyday things? Because if it’s something specific, maybe I could think about it better, but just generally… I don’t know that I’d say yes strongly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused when you're reading or doing quiet activities by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen. Not every single time I read or do something quiet, but pretty often, especially if it’s for school or like my business assignments.\n\nLike if I’m in class, I can start out really trying to listen, and at first I’m following what the professor is saying. But then a few minutes later I’ll realize my brain kind of wandered off without me noticing. I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or what kind of orders I could make, or ideas for how I want something to look, and then suddenly I’m like, wait, what did they just say? And it’s not that I decided to stop listening, it just sort of happens.\n\nIt’s the same with videos I’m supposed to watch for assignments. I’ll sit down and press play and genuinely mean to pay attention, but then after a little bit I notice I missed a whole chunk of it. Sometimes I have to rewind because I realize I heard the sound but didn’t actually take in what they were explaining. It’s kind of frustrating because I’m physically there, and I’m not trying to ignore it, but my mind is already somewhere else making plans or coming up with ideas.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects that you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not always that I just abandon the whole thing, but I’ll start something and feel like I’m doing it, and then later realize I didn’t actually finish all the parts I was supposed to.\n\nLike if a teacher gives me a few steps for an assignment, I’ll do the first thing or the main part, and then I’ll find out I skipped something important, like attaching the form or turning in an extra piece that was part of the instructions. Or with vendor stuff, if I’m talking to someone about an order and they give me a couple of details to include, I’ll get the basic note down, but then later realize I didn’t finish the last part of the order note or left out something they specifically asked for.\n\nIt’s frustrating because in my head I feel like, “Okay, I started it, I’m handling it,” but then the follow-through part gets messy. I don’t always notice until someone points it out or I’m already past the point where it would’ve been easier to fix.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to stick with a task until it's fully completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I think that’s kind of one of the bigger problems for me, honestly. It’s like I can start something and I’ll feel like I’m doing it properly, but sticking with it all the way through every step is where it gets messy.\n\nLike if a teacher gives me a few things I’m supposed to do for an assignment, I’ll usually do the first part, or the part that feels most obvious, and then later I’ll find out I missed something important. Like I forgot to attach a form, or I didn’t do one of the extra pieces that was part of the instructions. And it’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, I don’t care about that part.” I just sort of move forward and then realize afterward that I didn’t actually finish the whole thing.\n\nIt happens with vendor stuff too. If I’m in a meeting or talking about an order and they give me several details, I’ll write down or remember the main thing, but then later I notice I didn’t finish the last part of the order note, or I left out something they specifically wanted included. So in my head it feels like I handled it, but then when I look back, it’s like… no, I only handled part of it. And that’s frustrating because it makes me feel careless even when I was trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like at home or in a library?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I notice it a lot when I’m supposed to be sitting still, especially if it’s quiet and there isn’t anything I’m actively doing with my hands or body.\n\nIt’s hard to explain, but even if I’m not actually going anywhere or needing to do anything, my body feels like it’s buzzing almost. Like there’s this feeling inside me that I should be moving somehow. If I’m sitting and listening, I’ll feel like I need to shift around, bounce my leg, move my hands, adjust how I’m sitting, anything. And if I try not to, then I’m just thinking about how badly I want to move, so it’s not really restful.\n\nIt happens at home too, not just in public. Like if I’m trying to sit quietly or someone is talking and I’m supposed to just listen, I can feel myself getting restless even if nothing is actually wrong. It’s not always that I get up and leave, but I feel like I have all this energy trapped in my body and I need to do something with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble controlling your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, I have emotions like anyone, and sometimes I get upset or frustrated, but I don’t know if I’d say I “often” have trouble controlling them.\n\nMaybe it depends what you mean by controlling them? Like, if you mean do I have huge outbursts all the time or I can’t calm down at all, I don’t think that’s really me. But if you mean do I sometimes feel something strongly and it shows on my face or in my tone before I’ve fully thought it through, then… maybe sometimes? I don’t know if that’s more than normal, though.\n\nI guess I haven’t really paid attention to it as a main problem. I’d probably need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting overly emotional or reactive to situations?\n\nPatient: I’m still not completely sure, honestly. “Overly emotional” is hard for me to judge because I don’t always know what the normal amount of reaction is supposed to be in a situation.\n\nI don’t think I’m constantly having big reactions or anything like that. Like, I wouldn’t say people are always telling me I’m too emotional or that I’m blowing things out of proportion. But sometimes if something catches me off guard or frustrates me, I might react kind of quickly in the moment before I’ve had time to think it through. Then afterward I might be like, okay, maybe I sounded more irritated than I meant to, or maybe I took that more personally than I needed to.\n\nBut I don’t know if that happens often enough that I’d call it a major problem. It’s not really something I’ve tracked. I guess I’d say… maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d describe myself as often overly emotional or reactive.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering away from the task at hand when you're supposed to be focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. I can start out really trying to pay attention, like in class or if I’m watching a video for one of my business assignments, and at first I’m listening and following along. But then after a few minutes, it’s like my brain just slips off somewhere else without me deciding to.\n\nI’ll suddenly be thinking about charm designs, or ideas for orders, or like what colors would look good together, or how I could organize something for that. And it’s not always totally random stuff, it’s just not what I’m supposed to be focusing on at that moment.\n\nThen I kind of snap back and realize the video has kept going or the teacher has been talking, and I missed a whole chunk of what was said. Sometimes it feels like I was technically sitting there the whole time, looking like I was paying attention, but I wasn’t really taking it in. So then I have to rewind the video or try to piece together what I missed, which is frustrating because I did sit down intending to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick with one activity for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can stick with something if it’s really grabbing me, but if it’s something like class or a video I’m supposed to watch for my business assignments, it gets hard pretty quickly.\n\nI’ll start off fine, like I’m listening and trying to follow what they’re saying, and then after a few minutes I realize my brain has gone somewhere else completely. Not even always somewhere random — it’ll be like charm designs, or ideas for orders, or colors I want to use, or something related to that. And then I’ll kind of come back and notice the video is still going or the teacher has moved on, and I missed like half of what was said.\n\nSo it’s not always that I get up and stop the activity physically, but mentally I’m not really staying with it for that long. I have to rewind things or try to catch back up, and it can make something take way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to maintain your focus on a single task for as long as it requires to be completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. That’s probably one of the more noticeable things for me, especially with school-type stuff or anything I have to sit through until it’s done.\n\nLike if I’m in class, or I’m watching a video for my business assignments, I can start out actually listening. I’m not trying to ignore it. I’ll be like, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I need this information. But then after a few minutes, my mind just kind of drifts off before I even realize it’s happening. I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or order ideas, or what colors I want to use for something, and it feels like I’ve gone down this whole separate train of thought.\n\nThen I’ll suddenly notice the teacher is still talking, or the video has kept playing, and I’ve missed half of what was said. Sometimes I’m still looking at the screen, so it feels weird because physically I’m there, but mentally I’m not really following it anymore. Then I have to rewind or try to figure out what I missed, which makes the whole task take longer than it should. So yeah, maintaining focus all the way until something is finished is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your attention on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I know I kind of said this already, but it’s really like… I can begin something with good intentions, like in class or watching one of the videos I have to watch for my business assignments, and for the first few minutes I’m actually there. I’m listening, I’m following what’s being said, I’m trying to take it in.\n\nBut then after a little bit, my attention just sort of slides away. I’ll suddenly be thinking about charm designs, or ideas for orders, or colors I want to use, and I don’t even always notice right when it happens. It’s like I’m still sitting there and maybe even looking at the screen, but my brain has gone somewhere completely different.\n\nThen I’ll snap back and realize I missed a big part of what the teacher said, or half the video went by and I don’t really know what happened. So keeping my attention on one thing for a long time is hard, especially if it’s something I have to just sit and listen to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with friendships, work, or other activities, because of your organizational issues or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has started causing real problems, not just like “oh, I’m a little scattered.” The biggest thing is with my program. There were required steps I was supposed to keep up with, and I missed some of them because I wasn’t organized about it and didn’t stay on top of what needed to be done. And now my financial aid is at risk, which is honestly really stressful because it feels like one of those things where I knew it mattered, but I still didn’t manage it the way I should have.\n\nAnd then with shop projects, it’s affected things too. Like people I work with have started depending on me less, I think because they’re not always sure I’m going to follow through smoothly or keep track of everything. That feels embarrassing, because I do care about the work and I don’t want people to feel like they can’t count on me. But I can tell it’s changed a little, like they’ll take over parts or not ask me for as much, and I can’t really blame them, but it still feels bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulties with organization, restlessness, or completing tasks affect your performance or relationships not just in school or your shop, but also in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the biggest examples are still the school program and the shop stuff, because those are where it became really obvious and where there were actual consequences.\n\nWith my program, I missed required steps that I was supposed to keep up with, and now my financial aid is at risk, which feels like a really big deal. It’s not just me being messy or forgetting something small — it’s like, okay, this could actually affect whether I can continue the way I planned. And that’s been stressful because I knew those things mattered, but I still didn’t stay organized enough to handle them properly.\n\nAnd with shop projects, it’s affected relationships too, I guess. People I work with have started depending on me less, like they don’t fully trust that I’ll keep track of everything or finish my part smoothly. I can kind of feel them pulling back or taking over things, and that’s embarrassing because I really do care. I don’t want to seem unreliable. So yeah, even if I mostly notice it in those areas, it does affect how people see me and how much they count on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still, fidgeting, and restlessness are as bothersome to you as your difficulties with organization, completing tasks, and staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the organization and finishing things causes more obvious problems, like consequences people can point to. So yeah, even if I mostly notice it in those areas, it does affect how people see me and how much they count on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still, fidgeting, and restlessness are as bothersome to you as your difficulties with organization, completing tasks, and staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the organization and finishing things causes more obvious problems, like consequences people can point to. But the restlessness is really bothersome too, just in a different way.\n\nIt’s like even when I’m not actually going anywhere or doing anything, my body feels kind of… buzzy? Like there’s this energy under my skin and I’m supposed to move somehow. Especially if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m expected to just be still and listen, it gets really uncomfortable. I’ll feel like I need to shift around or do something with my hands or change position, and if I try not to, then I’m focusing on trying to stay still instead of whatever I’m supposed to be listening to.\n\nSo I guess the organization stuff messes things up more on the outside, but the restlessness is more like this constant inside irritation that makes sitting through things feel harder than it probably should."
